consistent with the transformations (
3.2.3
). This leads to ambiguities in the description of the post-transformation state, and, hence, in the definition of the transformation itself, which we now attempt to resolve.

In light of our previous considerations, two natural candidates for the definition of the state
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
are immediately apparent.
The first of these, which we denote
\ket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
, is defined in the position-basis by performing the coordinate transformation
{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf x}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{A}
,
{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
on the state
\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}
(
3.12
).
The momentum-basis expansion is then defined, with respect to this, so as to implement the transformation
\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto{\bf p}_{O}=-{\bf p}_{A}
,
\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
, while maintaining the basis-independence of the normalisation condition,
\braket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}|{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}=1
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\ket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

(3.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}({\bf r}_{A})\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})\ket{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\ket{{\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{A})\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(\bm{\kappa}_{A})\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{OB}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}\,,

where

\displaystyle\bra{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\bra{{\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}}_{B}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{OB}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{6}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}(\bm{\kappa}_{A}.{\bf r}_{A}+\bm{\kappa}_{B}.{\bf r}_{B})\right]\,,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{6}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\left\{{\bf p}_{O}.{\bf x}_{O}+({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}).({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\right\}\right]\,,

(3.30)

and

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf x}_{O}|-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}=\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{O}+{\bf r}_{A})\,,\quad\braket{{\bf x}_{B}|{\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}}_{B}=\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{B}-({\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}))\,,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}|\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{OB}:=\delta^{3}({\bf p}_{O}+\bm{\kappa}_{A})\,\delta^{3}({\bf p}_{B}-(\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}))\,.

(3.32)

The second option, which we denote
\ket{{}^{({\rm p})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
, is defined in the momentum-basis by performing the coordinate transformation
\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto{\bf p}_{O}=-{\bf p}_{A}
,
\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
on the state
\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}
(
3.12
).
The position-basis expansion is then defined, with respect to this, so as to implement the transformation
{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf x}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{A}
,
{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
, while maintaining the basis-independence of the condition
\braket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}|{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}=1
.
This gives

\displaystyle\ket{{}^{(p)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{O})\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

(3.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(\bm{\kappa}_{A})\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})\ket{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}({\bf r}_{A})\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})\ket{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\ket{{\bf r}_{A}\,{\bf r}_{B}}_{OB}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}\,,

where

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf r}_{A}\,{\bf r}_{B}}_{OB}\ket{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{B}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{6}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}(\bm{\kappa}_{A}.{\bf r}_{A}+\bm{\kappa}_{B}.{\bf r}_{B})\right]\,,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\bra{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{6}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\left\{{\bf p}_{O}.{\bf x}_{O}+({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}).({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\right\}\right]\,.

(3.35)

and

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}|{\bf r}_{A}\,{\bf r}_{B}}_{OB}:=\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{O}+{\bf r}_{A})\,\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{B}-({\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}))\,,

(3.36)

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf p}_{O}|-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}=\delta^{3}({\bf p}_{O}+\bm{\kappa}_{A})\,,\quad\braket{{\bf p}_{B}|\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{B}=\delta^{3}({\bf p}_{B}-(\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}))\,.

(3.37)

Here, we have used the cumbersome notation
\ket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
to indicate that the position-basis utilised in the expansion of the state (
3.28
) is unentangled, whereas the corresponding momentum-basis is entangled according to Eqs. (
3.29
)-(
3.32
).
Likewise,
\ket{{}^{({\rm p})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
indicates that the momentum-basis utilised in the expansion of the state (
3.33
) is unentangled, whereas the corresponding position-basis expansion is entangled according to Eqs. (
3.34
)-(
3.37
).
Notationally, we indicate the non-separability of the bases
\ket{{\bf r}_{A}\,{\bf r}_{B}}_{OB}
,
\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}
and
\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{OB}
,
\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}
by not separating the variables enclosed within the ket by a comma,
\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}\neq\ket{{\bf x}_{O},{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}:=\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}
, etc.

If we choose
\ket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
to represent the state of the ‘bipartite’ system
OB
, the associated density operator is

\displaystyle{}^{({\rm x})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}:=\ket{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}\bra{{}^{({\rm x})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\,.

(3.38)

We may obtain the desired probability densities, for both position and momentum measurements, by acting on this matrix with the projection operators

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}}:=\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}\,,\quad\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}}:=\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}\,,

(3.39)

respectively, before tracing over the
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}
subspace of the full Hilbert space,
\mathcal{H}_{OB}^{(A)}=\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}}{}^{({\rm x})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}=|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

(3.40)

and

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}}{}^{({\rm x})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,,

(3.41)

as required.

Similarly, if we choose
\ket{{}^{({\rm p})}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
to represent the state of
OB
, as seen by
A
, we must instead define the density operator

\displaystyle{}^{({\rm p})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}:=\ket{{}^{(p)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\bra{{}^{(p)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\,.

(3.42)

To obtain the required position and momentum probability densities, we must act on this with the projection operators

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}}:=\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}\,,\quad\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}}:=\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}\,,

(3.43)

respectively, before performing the partial trace over
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}
, giving

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}}{}^{({\rm p})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}=|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

(3.44)

and

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}}{}^{({\rm p})}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,.

(3.45)

However, it is most convenient to express the state of
O
and Bob, from Alice’s perspective, in terms of the symmetric bases
\left\{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\right\}
and
\left\{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\right\}
, as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

(3.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\psi_{O}({\bf r}_{A})\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})\ket{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\ket{{\bf r}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{O})\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{O}(\bm{\kappa}_{A})\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})\ket{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}\,.

The corresponding density matrix is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}:=\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\bra{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\,,

(3.47)

and we must act on this with the projection operators

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}}:=\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}}:=\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\,,

(3.48)

before performing the partial trace over
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}
.
These operations give

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}=|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

(3.49)

and

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,,

(3.50)

as required, since the symmetrised position and momentum basis vectors form canonical-type brakets, according to

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf r}_{A}|\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{3}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,.\,{\bf r}_{A}\right]\,,\quad\braket{{\bf r}_{B}|\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{3}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\bm{\kappa}_{B}\,.\,{\bf r}_{B}\right]\,,

(3.51)

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf x}_{O}|{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{3}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}{\bf p}_{O}\,.\,{\bf x}_{O}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}|{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\right)^{3}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\,.\,({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\right]\,,

(3.52)

and

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf x}_{O}|-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}=\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{O}+{\bf r}_{A})\,,\quad\braket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}|{\bf r}_{B}}_{B}=\delta^{3}(({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})-{\bf r}_{B})\,,

(3.53)

\displaystyle\braket{{\bf p}_{O}|-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}=\delta^{3}({\bf p}_{O}+\bm{\kappa}_{A})\,,\quad\braket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}|\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}=\delta^{3}(({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})-\bm{\kappa}_{B})\,.

(3.54)

After all this work, we have finally obtained our desired result; a concrete model of the transition from a classical frame of reference,
O
, to the quantum frame of reference corresponding to Alice’s system.
For the bipartite quantum system
AB
, the probability that Alice perceives Bob’s state as the state
\ket{\varphi_{B}^{(A)}}_{B}
is given by the map
S^{(O\rightarrow A)}_{OAB}(\varphi_{B}^{(A)}):\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\mapsto{\rm d}^{3}P(\varphi_{B}^{(A)}|\Psi_{AB}^{(O)})
, which is defined by the relation

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P(\varphi_{B}^{(A)}|\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}):=S^{(O\rightarrow A)}_{OAB}(\varphi_{B}^{(A)})\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}

(3.55)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob}=\varphi_{B}^{(A)}}_{OB}}\,\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\bra{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob}=\varphi_{B}^{(A)}}_{OB}}\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}\,,

where
\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob}=\varphi_{B}^{(A)}}_{OB}}
is an appropriate projection operator, that acts on the total Hilbert space of the composite state, and
{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}
is the corresponding volume element for Bob’s degrees of freedom (e.g.,
{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}={\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}
(
3.49
) or
{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}={\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}
(
3.50
), as in the examples above).
In general, this may be a projection onto an entangled basis of the
OB
system,
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

Clearly, (
3.55
) does not define a single map, but a family of projection-dependent maps.
To our horror, we seem to have encountered a similar problem to that faced in
[
14
]
, namely, a basis-dependent ambiguity in the definition of the post-transformation state.
But this is not really the case.
The predictions of our QRF model depend on the chosen projector,
\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob}=\varphi_{B}^{(A)}}_{OB}}
, but not on the basis used to express the ‘objective’ state,
\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}
.
This is as it should be, since the former is determined by the the kind of physical measurements we choose to make on the system, whereas the latter is not.
In order to extract predictions for the results of position measurements, we must project onto the basis
\left\{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\right\}
, which implements the coordinate transformation
{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf x}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{A}
,
{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
, whereas, in order to extract predictions for the results of momentum measurements, we must project onto the basis
\left\{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\right\}
, which is equivalent to implementing the coordinate transformation
\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto{\bf p}_{A}=-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
,
\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
.
Crucially, we see that these position and momentum space transformations are not mutually exclusive.
There exists a single bipartite state,
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}:=\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}
(
3.46
), that is compatible with
both
transformations, (
3.2.3
).

In a completely analogous way, we can define a map from the CRF
O
to the QRF
B
, for the bipartite
AB
system, as

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P(\varphi_{A}^{(B)}|\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}):=S^{(O\rightarrow B)}_{OAB}(\varphi_{A}^{(B)})\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}

(3.56)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Alice}=\varphi_{A}^{(B)}}_{AO}}\,\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow B}\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\bra{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Alice}=\varphi_{A}^{(B)}}_{AO}}\hat{\rho}_{AO}^{(B)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm V}_{\varphi}\,,

where
\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Alice}=\varphi_{A}^{(B)}}_{AO}}
is an appropriate projection operator, that acts on the total Hilbert space of the composite state,
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(B)}:=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{B\leftrightarrow O}\mathcal{H}_{B}
, and
{\rm d}^{3}\varphi_{A}^{(B)}
denotes the corresponding volume element for Alice’s degrees of freedom.
By projecting onto the basis
\left\{\ket{{\bf q}_{A}-{\bf q}_{O}}_{A}\ket{{\bf q}_{O}}_{O}\right\}
, we obtain the predictions of our model for the outcomes of position measurements, viewed from
B
’s perspective.
In like manner, projecting onto the basis
\left\{\ket{\bm{\pi}_{A}-\bm{\pi}_{O}}_{A}\ket{\bm{\pi}_{O}}_{O}\right\}
yields predictions for the outcomes of momentum measurements, as seen by
B
’s QRF.
However, in general, we may determine the probability that Alice’s effective state, as seen by Bob, is an arbitrary wave function, denoted
\varphi_{A}^{(B)}
.

From these results, we see immediately that the CRF-to-QRF transition is
non-unitary
.
This makes intuitive sense, since, although the CRF
O
has access to complete information about the bipartite quantum system
AB
, the information that can be obtained by either subspace,
A
or
B
, is limited to measurements of their
relative
position and momentum.
By ‘jumping’ from a CRF to a QRF, therefore, information is effectively destroyed, in the sense that it become unavailable to the designated ‘observer’.

3.2.4
The non-unitary nature of QRF to QRF transitions

Moreover, since the trace over the effective subspace attributed to
O
is performed
after
the parity swap operation, the transformation from Alice’s QRF to Bob’s QRF must also be represented by a non-unitary operation.
When performing the transition CRF
O\rightarrow
QRF
A
, we trace over the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}:=\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\mathcal{H}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{A}
of the total
AB
Hilbert space,

\mathcal{H}_{AB}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, whereas, when performing the transition CRF
O\rightarrow
QRF
B
, we trace over
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(B)}:=\hat{P}_{O\leftrightarrow B}\mathcal{H}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
Each operation renders certain information about the total state physically inaccessible, but the information that is effectively destroyed in each case is different, since there is no a priori relation between the wave functions of
A
and
B
.
We discuss the physical implications of this, in greater detail, in Sec.
3.2.5
.

3.2.5
Generalised uncertainty relations

To illustrate the physical consequences of the conclusions drawn in Sec.
3.2.3
, regarding the non-unitary nature of the CRF-to-QRF transition, we now consider the uncertainty relations corresponding to Alice’s measurements of Bob’s relative position and momentum.
To proceed, it is convenient to define the generalised position and momentum operators,
\hat{{\bf X}}_{B}
and
\hat{{\bf P}}_{B}
, whose eigenvalues are
{\bf x}_{B}
and
{\bf p}_{B}
, respectively.
Here, we use capital letters to emphasise the fact that these operators act on the tensor product Hilbert space,
\mathcal{H}_{OB}^{(A)}=\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, and not on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
alone.
The components of these operators are

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{B}^{i}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B}^{i}+\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B}^{\prime i}

(3.57)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{O}^{i}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{B}+\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{O}\otimes\widehat{x_{B}^{i}-x_{O}^{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int x_{O}^{i}\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\int(x_{B}^{i}-x_{O}^{i})\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(-\hat{r}_{A}^{i})_{O}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{B}+\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{O}\otimes\hat{r}_{B}^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int(-r_{A}^{i})\ket{-{\bf r}_{A}}\bra{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf r}_{B}}\bra{{\bf r}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\int r_{B}^{i}\ket{-{\bf r}_{A}}\bra{-{\bf r}_{A}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf r}_{B}}\bra{{\bf r}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}\,,

and

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{Bj}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{Bj}+\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{Bj}^{\prime}

(3.58)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{Oj}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{B}+\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{O}\otimes\widehat{p_{Bj}-p_{Oj}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int p_{Oj}\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\int(p_{Bj}-p_{Oj})\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(-\hat{\kappa}_{Aj})_{O}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{B}+\hat{\mathbb{I}}_{O}\otimes\hat{\kappa}_{Bj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int(-\kappa_{Aj})\ket{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}\bra{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}\otimes\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}\bra{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\int\kappa_{Bj}\ket{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}\bra{-\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{O}\otimes\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}\bra{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}\,.

Using these definitions, plus the definition of the state
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
(
3.46
), it follows immediately that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Psi}X_{B}^{i}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\Psi}\mathcal{X}_{B}^{i})^{2}+(\Delta_{\Psi}\mathcal{X}_{B}^{\prime i})^{2}}\equiv\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i})^{2}+(\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i})^{2}}\,,

(3.59)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Psi}P_{Bj}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\Psi}\mathcal{P}_{Bj})^{2}+(\Delta_{\Psi}\mathcal{P}_{Bj}^{\prime})^{2}}\equiv\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj})^{2}+(\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj})^{2}}\,,

(3.60)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i}\,\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj}\geq\frac{\hbar}{2}\,\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,,\quad\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i}\,\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj}\geq\frac{\hbar}{2}\,\delta^{i}{}_{j}

(3.61)

are the fundamental uncertainty relations for the wave functions
\psi_{A}
and
\phi_{B}
.
Here, the subscript
\rho
denotes the effective state of Bob’s system, as seen by Alice, which may be mixed rather than pure.
(We consider this scenario, in greater detail, in Sec.
3.2.6
.)

The important point is that,
if
a real physical observer could be embodied as a classical object - that is, as a material body that is perfectly well localised in both position and momentum space - they would be able to measure not only the relative position and momentum of Alice and Bob, but also their ‘objective’ positions and momenta, relative to a classical frame of reference defined with respect to the background geometry.
In other words, they would be able to measure the quantities
{\bf r}_{A}
and
{\bf r}_{B}
, or
\bm{\kappa}_{A}
and
\bm{\kappa}_{B}
, independently, and, thus, to determine the individual uncertainties

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i}\,,\quad\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i}\,,

(3.62)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj}\,,\quad\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj}\,,

(3.63)

by direct measurement.
By contrast, in the bipartite
AB
system, Alice can only perform physical measurements to determine the quantities
{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
and
{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
.
She is able to calculate the generalised uncertainties
\Delta_{\Psi}X_{B}^{i}\equiv\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}
(
3.59
) by performing repeated measurements of Bob’s position on an ensemble of identically prepared experiments - taking care not to alter the preparation of her own state, too - but she cannot determine either
\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i}
or
\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i}
in this way.
Similarly, she is able to calculate the generalised uncertainties
\Delta_{\Psi}P_{Bj}\equiv\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}
(
3.60
) by performing repeated measurements of his relative momentum, in like manner, but she cannot determine either
\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj}
or
\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj}
using this procedure.

Thus, she is unable to detect her own ‘quantumness’, from the result of any individual measurement, or from the statistical spreads of any one set of measurements, of either
{\bf x}_{B}
or
{\bf p}_{B}
.
Nonetheless, as we will now show, Alice
is
able to infer limited information about her own quantum state, using the generalised uncertainty relations implied by Eqs. (
3.59
)-(
3.60
).
To construct these, we combine both equations to give two alternative expressions, which together contain the same information as (
3.59
)-(
3.60
), namely

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sigma_{A}^{i})^{2}\,(\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj})^{2}+(\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Aj})^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(\sigma_{A}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Aj})^{2}-(\sigma_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Bj})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sigma_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj})^{2}+(\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Bj})^{2}

(3.64)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(\sigma_{B}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Bj})^{2}-(\sigma_{A}^{i})^{2}\,(\tilde{\sigma}_{Aj})^{2}\,,

where we have rewritten

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i}=\sigma_{A}^{i}\,,\quad\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj}=\tilde{\sigma}_{Aj}\,,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i}=\sigma_{B}^{i}\,,\quad\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj}=\tilde{\sigma}_{Bj}\,.

(3.65)

This new notation is intended to emphasise the fact that it is not possible to determine the uncertainties
\Delta_{\psi}r_{A}^{i}
and
\Delta_{\phi}r_{B}^{i}
, individually, from the statistical spread of position measurements performed by any
physical
observer.
Likewise, it is not possible to determine either
\Delta_{\psi}\kappa_{Aj}
or
\Delta_{\phi}\kappa_{Bj}
, individually, from the statistical spread of physical momentum measurements.
In the GURs (
3.2.5
), these quantities therefore play the role of numerical coefficients, multiplying the empirically measurable quantities
\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}
and
\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}
.
By combining her data on both position and momentum measurements, and using these relations, Alice is, at least in principle, able to determine the values of
\sigma_{A}^{i}
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{Aj}
,
\sigma_{B}^{i}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{Bj}
, for a given ensemble of identically prepared systems.
She is therefore able to determine her own position and momentum uncertainties, albeit
indirectly
, via their effect on the uncertainty relations she attributes to Bob.
If the latter are found to deviate from the canonical Heisenberg form, according to Eqs. (
3.2.5
), then Alice is able to determine that both she and Bob are quantum mechanical in nature.
The product of uncertainties
\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}\,\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}
remains bounded by the Schrödinger-Robertson limit,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}\,\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}\geq\frac{(\hbar+\hbar)}{2}\,\delta^{i}{}_{j}=\hbar\,\delta^{i}{}_{j}\,,

(3.66)

and this inequality is saturated when either

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}^{i}\tilde{\sigma}_{Ai}=\frac{\hbar}{2}\,;\quad\sigma_{B}^{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{\sigma_{A}^{i}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{Ai}}}\,,\ \tilde{\sigma}_{Bi}=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{Ai}}{\sigma_{A}^{i}}}\,,

(3.67)

or

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}^{i}=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{\sigma_{B}^{i}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{Bi}}}\,,\ \tilde{\sigma}_{Ai}=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2}\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{Bi}}{\sigma_{B}^{i}}}\,;\quad\sigma_{B}^{i}\tilde{\sigma}_{Bi}=\frac{\hbar}{2}\,.

(3.68)

This situation is analogous to that proposed in phenomenological quantum gravity, in which the quantised spatial background is expected to act, effectively, as a non-material QRF, giving rise to GURs like the GUP, EUP or EGUP, which have been extensively studied in the literature
[
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
]
.
In this scenario, it is impossible to detect the quantum nature of spacetime, directly, through its effects on any individual measurement of a material system.
Nonetheless, it is still possible to infer the existence of quantum gravitational effects - that is, effects due to the quantum nature of the background geometry -
indirectly
, via their induced modifications of the Heisenberg uncertainty principle
[
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
]
.
We discuss this point, in greater detail, in Sec.
5
.

Finally, before concluding this section, we note that using the ‘sub-operators’
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B}^{i}
,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B}^{\prime i}
and
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{Bj}
,
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{Bj}
, defined in in Eqs. (
3.57
)-(
3.58
), we can construct the useful unitary operators

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime(A\rightarrow O)}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{A\leftrightarrow O}\,\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime(A)}\,,\quad\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime(A)}:=\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{{\bf\mathcal{P}}}^{\prime}_{B}\,.\,\hat{{\bf\mathcal{X}}}_{B}\right]\,,

(3.69)

and

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime\prime(A\rightarrow O)}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{A\leftrightarrow O}\,\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)}\,,\quad\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)}:=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{{\bf\mathcal{P}}}_{B}\,.\,\hat{{\bf\mathcal{X}}}_{B}^{\prime}\right]\,.

(3.70)

Although we use very different notation, the operators
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime(A\rightarrow O)}
and
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime\prime(A\rightarrow O)}
are completely equivalent to the operators that would be denoted as
\hat{S}_{x}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
and
\hat{S}_{p}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
, respectively, in the GCB formalism.
(In which
O
would be regarded as a
genuine
quantum system.)
In our notation, the corresponding transitions between states are expressed as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{\prime(A)}}_{OB}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime(A\rightarrow O)\dagger}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime\prime(A\rightarrow O)\dagger}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)}}_{OB}\,,

(3.71)

where
\ket{\Psi_{AB}^{(O)}}_{AB}
is the state of the bipartite quantum system
AB
, as seen by the classical observer
O
(
3.12
), and the states
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{\prime(A)}}_{OB}
and
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)}}_{OB}
are defined in Eqs. (
3.16
) and (
3.23
).
We recall that these states are our failed candidates, for the state of the
OB
system, as nominally seen by Alice, and that each was rejected on the grounds that it is not compatible with the required coordinate transformations.

In the GCB model, both operators,
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime(A\rightarrow O)}\equiv\hat{S}_{x}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
and
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime\prime(A\rightarrow O)}\equiv\hat{S}_{p}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
, are associated with a single transition between two QRFs and it is stated that “whenever we perform a transformation to a quantum reference frame, we have to choose the relative variables we are interested in, and then complete the transformation of the conjugated variable by canonicity”
[
14
]
.
As we have shown above, this is not the only possibility, and we can remove the ambiguity in the definitions of both CRF-to-QRF and QRF-to-QRF transitions by choosing an appropriate set of basis states, which are compatible with the canonical coordinate transformations we require, Eqs. (
3.2.3
).

Nonetheless, it turns out that much of the mathematical machinery, originally developed in
[
14
]
, is still useful in our model.
In particular, the operators
\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime(A)}
and
\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)}
, which act on the ‘bipartite’ state
OB
and are simply
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime(A\rightarrow O)}\equiv\hat{S}_{x}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
and
\hat{U}_{OAB}^{\prime\prime(A\rightarrow O)}\equiv\hat{S}_{p}^{(A\rightarrow O)}
, without the parity swap
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{A\leftrightarrow O}
, transform between the entangled and separable bases, introduced in Eqs. (
3.28
), (
3.33
) and (
3.46
), according to

\displaystyle\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}\,,\,\ket{{\bf p}_{O}\,{\bf p}_{B}}_{OB}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime(A)\dagger}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\,,\,\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}

\displaystyle\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\,,\,\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)\dagger}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\ket{{\bf x}_{O}\,{\bf x}_{B}}_{OB}\,,\,\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}\,.

(3.72)

They therefore transform between our alternative ‘candidates’ for the state of the
OB
system, as seen by Alice, according to

\displaystyle\ket{{}^{(x)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime(A)\dagger}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{U}_{OB}^{\prime\prime(A)\dagger}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\ket{{}^{(p)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}\,.

(3.73)

However, here, the states
\ket{{}^{(x)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
(
3.28
),
\ket{{}^{(p)}\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
(
3.46
) and
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}_{OB}
(
3.33
), are not really alternative candidates for the state of
OB
.
We have shown that the map between a CRF and a QRF, as well as the map between two QRFs, is necessarily non-unitary.
In our formalism, the unitary equivalence between these states indicates their
physical equivalence
, as descriptions of the
OB
system, viewed from Alice’s perspective.
As shown, explicitly, in Sec.
3.2.3
, all three states are compatible with the same set of canonical coordinate transformations, Eqs. (
3.2.3
), and give rise to the same predictions for the probability densities associated with measurements of
{\bf x}_{B}
or
{\bf p}_{B}
, following the partial trace over
O
’s
effective
degrees of freedom.

3.2.6
Phase information

So far, we have considered only the squared absolute value of Bob’s effective state, as seen by Alice.
This was all that we required, in order to determine the probabilities associated with measurements of their relative position or momentum,
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})=|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}
and
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}
.
Now, in order to complete our description of Alice’s QRF, we must consider what information she is able to obtain about the phase distribution of Bob’s state.

To proceed, let us first consider the position space representation.
Again, the key point is that a single value of the relative displacement,
{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
, corresponds to multiple pairings
({\bf r}_{A},{\bf r}_{B})
, and hence to multiple points in Bob’s wave function
\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})=|\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})|e^{i\theta_{B}({\bf r}_{B})}\equiv\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})=|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})|e^{i\theta_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})}
.
Generically, these points will have both different phases and different absolute values of the amplitude density,

\displaystyle\theta_{B}({\bf r}_{B})=\theta_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\,,\quad|\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})|=|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})|\,.

(3.74)

Bob’s pure state therefore appears, to Alice, as a mixed state.

To determine it’s form, let us consider a measurement that collapses Alice’s wave function to a single position eigenstate,
{\bf r}_{A}
, but leaves Bob’s wave function unchanged.
Experimentally, we may imagine interfering Bob’s state with a given reference state,
R
, with known phase and absolute modulus distributions, i.e., a known wave function
\psi_{R}=|\psi_{R}|e^{i\theta_{R}}
.

13
13

13

This is clearly an idealised scenario, that is not, strictly speaking, ever reachable in practice. However, we may consider a state
\psi_{R}
that has been pre-prepared by a macroscopic,
effectively
classical observer (see Sec.
1.1.3
), whose knowledge has then been imparted to Alice.

This gives rise to a fixed interference pattern, and Alice may then ask the question, ‘how far away am I from a given point in this pattern?’
Once her displacement from one point in the pattern is fixed, her displacement from every other point is also fixed, and this fixes her relative displacement from each and every point in Bob’s wave function,
\phi_{B}
.
In this sense, she ‘sees’ Bob’s wave function, as whole, including all relevant phase and modulus information, that is also available to the CRF
O
.
In fact,
if
, by chance, Alice’s wave function collapses to the point
{\bf r}_{A}=0
, her perception of Bob’s state is identical
O
’s,
\ket{\phi_{B}}_{B}
.
For an arbitrary displacement,
{\bf r}_{A}\neq 0
, she instead sees Bob’s state translated by the vector
{\bf x}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{A}
, which we denote as
\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}
.
In this notation,
\ket{\phi_{B}}_{B}\equiv\ket{\phi_{B}(0)}_{B}
, and the general displaced state is defined as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}:=\hat{U}_{B}({\bf x}_{O})\ket{\phi_{B}}_{B}:=\int\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

(3.75)

where

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{B}({\bf x}_{O}):=\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{\mathbb{p}}_{B}\,.\,\mathbb{x}_{O}\right)

(3.76)

is the canonical displacement operator.
Bob’s mixed state, as seen by Alice, is therefore described by the density operator

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}}\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,,

(3.77)

where we have used the shorthand notation

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf r}_{A}|\Psi_{AB}^{(O)})={\rm d}^{3}P(-{\bf x}_{O}|\Psi_{OB}^{(A)})

(3.78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf r}_{A}|\psi_{A})={\rm d}^{3}P(-{\bf x}_{O}|\psi_{O})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\psi_{A}({\bf r}_{A})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}=|\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,.

To recap; in order to see that Eq. (
3.77
) is the correct description of Bob’s state, as seen by Alice, we need only remark that the same value of relative position,
{\bf x}_{B}
, can correspond to
any
individual values of
{\bf r}_{A}
and
{\bf r}_{B}
for which the relation
{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
holds.
In general, there will be an infinite number of pairs
({\bf r}_{A},{\bf r}_{B})=(-{\bf x}_{O},{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})
for which this relation is satisfied, and each individual pair can, in principle, correspond to both a different value of Bob’s phase, and a different value of his wave function’s absolute magnitude (
3.74
).
From Alice’s perspective, it appears that a different amplitude,
\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B})
, is associated with the same point in physical space, in each individual state of an ensemble of identically prepared systems; or, in other words, that Bob’s state is mixed.
This scenario is depicted, heuristically, in Fig. 5.

Using the definitions (
3.77
) and (
3.78
), we then obtain

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})={\rm tr}\left[\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}=|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

(3.79)

and

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})={\rm tr}\left[\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}=|\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,,

(3.80)

as required.
Finally, we are able to obtain the correct GUR,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rho}x_{B}^{i}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}x_{B}^{i})^{2}+(\Delta_{\phi}x_{B}^{i})^{2}}\,,

(3.81)

using the trace

\displaystyle\langle(\hat{x}_{B}^{i})^{n}\rangle_{\rho}={\rm tr}\left[(\hat{x}_{B}^{i})^{n}\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}\right]

(3.82)

where

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{B}^{i}=\int x_{B}^{i}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}

(3.83)

is the canonical one-particle position operator, without referring at all to the fictitious quantum subsystem,
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\mathcal{H}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
Unlike the operator
\hat{X}_{B}^{i}
(
3.57
), which acts on the state
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}\in\mathcal{H}_{OB}^{(A)}:=\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
(
3.46
), or, equivalently,
\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}
(
3.47
), the canonical position operator
\hat{x}_{B}^{i}
(
3.83
) acts directly on Bob’s effective mixed state,
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
), where
\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
(
3.75
).
The exact relation between
\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}
(
3.47
) and
\rho_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}:=\int{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\ket{\mathbb{x}_{O}}\bra{\mathbb{x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\hat{U}_{B}({\bf x}_{O})\,\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,.

(3.84)

Figure 5
:
The same relative displacement between Alice and Bob,
{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}
, can be associated with different values of Bob’s amplitude,
\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})=\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})
, depending on the position eigenstate,
{\bf r}_{A}=-{\bf x}_{O}
, that Alice’s wave function collapses to. Bob’s pure state therefore appears, to Alice, as a mixed state. In this diagram, the three solid black lines represent the same measured value of

{\bf x}_{B}
, but each corresponds to a different pair of values
({\bf r}_{A},{\bf r}_{B})
from the perspective of a classical observer
O
. An analogous diagram can also be constructed for the momentum space representation.

Analogous reasoning can also be applied to the momentum space picture, where
\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})=|\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})|e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})}\equiv\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})=|\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})|e^{i\theta_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})}
, and the same value of the relative momentum,
{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}
, can be associated with different values of the phase and absolute modulus of Bob’s momentum space wave function,

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})=\tilde{\theta}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\,,\quad|\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})|=|\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})|\,.

(3.85)

In this case, Bob’s state appears, to Alice, as the mixed state given by the density matrix

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}}\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,,

(3.86)

where the translated state is defined as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}_{B}:=\hat{\tilde{U}}_{B}({\bf p}_{O})\ket{\phi_{B}}_{B}:=\int\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,,

(3.87)

\hat{\tilde{U}}_{B}({\bf p}_{O})
is the momentum space translation operator,

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{U}}_{B}({\bf p}_{O}):=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\mathbb{p}_{O}\,.\,\hat{\mathbb{x}}_{B}\right)\,,

(3.88)

and we have introduced the shorthand notation

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P(\bm{\kappa}_{A}|\tilde{\Psi}_{AB}^{(O)})={\rm d}^{3}P(-{\bf p}_{O}|\tilde{\Psi}_{OB}^{(A)})

(3.89)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P(\bm{\kappa}_{A}|\tilde{\psi}_{A})={\rm d}^{3}P(-{\bf p}_{O}|\tilde{\psi}_{O})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{\psi}_{A}(\bm{\kappa}_{A})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{O})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,.

Experimentally, we may imagine performing a measurement that collapses Alice’s momentum, relative to
O
, to a particular eigenstate
\bm{\kappa}_{A}=-{\bf p}_{O}
, but leaves Bob’s wave function unchanged.
She may then employ the reference state
R
,
\tilde{\psi}_{R}=|\tilde{\psi}_{R}|e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{R}}=\mathcal{F}[\psi_{R}e^{i\theta_{R}}]
, where
\mathcal{F}
denotes the Fourier transform, to create a momentum space interference pattern.
This procedure fixes her distance, in momentum space, from each and every point in the momentum space representation of Bob’s wave function,
\tilde{\psi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})
.

Using the definitions (
3.86
)-(
3.89
), we then obtain

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})={\rm tr}\left[\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,,

(3.90)

and

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})={\rm tr}\left[\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{B})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{B})}_{B}\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{B})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,,

(3.91)

as required.
The required GUR for momentum measurements,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rho}p_{Bj}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}p_{Aj})^{2}+(\Delta_{\phi}p_{Bj})^{2}}\,,

(3.92)

is obtained from the trace

\displaystyle\langle(\hat{p}_{Bj})^{n}\rangle_{\rho}={\rm tr}\left[(\hat{p}_{Bj})^{n}\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}\right]\,,

(3.93)

where

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{Bj}=\int p_{Bj}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}

(3.94)

is the canonical one-particle momentum operator.
Again, in this formulation of our QRF model, we have no need to to refer to the fictitious quantum subsystem,
\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{O\leftrightarrow A}\mathcal{H}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
Unlike the operator
\hat{P}_{Bj}
(
3.58
), which acts on the state
\ket{\Psi_{OB}^{(A)}}\in\mathcal{H}_{OB}^{(A)}:=\mathcal{H}_{O}^{(A)}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
(
3.46
), or, equivalently,
\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}
(
3.47
), the canonical momentum operator
\hat{p}_{Bj}
(
3.94
) acts directly on Bob’s effective mixed state in momentum space,
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
), where
\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}_{B}\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
(
3.75
).
The exact relation between
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{OB}^{(A)}
(
3.47
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
) is

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}:=\int{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\ket{\mathbb{p}_{O}}\bra{\mathbb{p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\hat{\tilde{U}}_{B}({\bf p}_{O})\,\hat{\rho}_{OB}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,.

(3.95)

Yet again, we appear to have a similar problem to that encountered in the GCB formalism.
We have associated
two inequivalent mixed states
,
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
), which are related by
non-unitary
transformations, with the same QRF
A
, as opposed to associating this with
two inequivalent pure states
, which are related by
unitary
transformations, as in
[
14
]
.
However, in reality, there is no ambiguity.
As we have already shown, the mixed states
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
) yield the GURs (
3.81
) and (
3.92
), respectively.
In Sec.
3.2.5
, we showed how, by combining these relations, Alice is in principle able to determine that she, like Bob, is quantum mechanical in nature.
To do this, she must combine the statistical results obtained by performing repeated position measurements, on an ensemble of identically prepared systems, with the statistics obtained from repeated momentum measurements, performed on a second ensemble of states.

Performing only position measurements leads Alice to conclude that Bob’s state is the mixed state
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
), whereas performing only momentum measurements leads her to identify his state as
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
).
Only when she combines these two sets of complimentary results does she notice an
apparent
contradiction; the inequivalence of these two viewpoints alerts her to the fact that, in order to explain the observational facts, she must account, also, for the quantum mechanical nature of her own system.
This suggests an observational protocol, capable of distinguishing between competing definitions of the term ‘quantum reference frame’.

In summary, if the states
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
) are dissimilar enough for Alice to be able to detect a difference between the two, within the limits of her experimental precision, then she is able to discern the fact that she, like Bob, is a quantum system.
At the level of the uncertainty relations, this is equivalent to observing the non-Heisenberg statistics implied by Eqs. (
3.2.5
).
Such deviations from the canonical HUP do not appear to be possible within the standard QRF formalism
[
14
]
, so that designing an experiment to detect them should enable us to determine, once and for all, whether wave functions objectively exist in the physical background space
[
91
]
, and, hence, what the correct definition of a QRF should be.

These remarks complete our picture of Alice’s QRF, but the findings above beg an important question: If all physical observers are QRFs, and if all QRFs, like Alice, perceive pure states as mixed, then how can we reconcile this with the observation of pure states in physical experiments?
The answer, of course, is that the mixed nature of the states
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
) is obscured for observers with macroscopic proportions.
To see this, it is first easier to rewrite (
3.86
) in terms of velocities rather than momenta, using the relations

\displaystyle{\bf v}_{O}={\bf p}_{O}/M_{O}\,,\quad{\bf v}_{B}={\bf p}_{B}/m_{B}\,,

(3.96)

which are valid in the nonrelativistic approximation.
The corresponding uncertainty relations are then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}x_{O}^{i}\,\Delta_{\psi}v_{Oj}\geq\frac{\hbar}{2M_{O}}\,,\quad\Delta_{\psi}x_{B}^{i}\,\Delta_{\psi}v_{Bj}\geq\frac{\hbar}{2m_{B}}\,.

(3.97)

Here, we assume that the mass of the observed system, Bob, is very small compared to that of the observer, Alice,
m_{B}\ll M_{O}\equiv M_{A}
, and note that it is possible to obtain the limit

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}x_{O}^{i}\,\Delta_{\psi}v_{Oj}\rightarrow 0\,,\quad{\rm as}\quad M_{O}\rightarrow\infty\,.

(3.98)

This is compatible with the approximations

\displaystyle|\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}\simeq\delta^{3}(-{\bf x}_{O})\quad{\rm and}\quad|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf v}_{O})|^{2}\simeq\delta^{3}(-{\bf v}_{O})\,,

(3.99)

giving

\displaystyle\Delta_{\psi}x_{O}^{i}\simeq 0\quad{\rm and}\quad\Delta_{\psi}v_{Oj}\simeq 0\,,

(3.100)

individually, for large enough
M_{O}
.

Next consider, for example, the scenario in which the system corresponding to Alice’s QRF is not a microscopic particle, but a massive composite body consisting of many individual particles.
In this case, her position coordinate
{\bf r}_{A}=-{\bf x}_{O}
does not represent the position of a single particle, but the position of the centre-of-mass of the composite body, and
\bm{\kappa}_{A}=-{\bf p}_{O}=-M_{O}{\bf v}_{O}
is its conjugate momentum.
Now let us assume, for the sake of argument, that Alice’s wave function has a positional uncertainty of the order of an angstrom and that her mass is of the order of
\sim 100{\rm kg}
, which would not be unreasonable for a realistic piece of laboratory equipment, or for the human observer operating it.
Her corresponding velocity uncertainty is of the order of
\sim 10^{-26}

{\rm ms^{-1}}
.

14
14

14

For context, we note that, if this were a true velocity, rather than the standard deviation of a statistical distribution, it would take an object travelling at this speed roughly the age of the Universe,
t_{\rm U}\simeq 10^{17}
s, to traverse the diameter of a single atom.

In short, her wave function is extremely narrow, in both position space and velocity space, due to the large value of her mass, so that both
|\psi_{O}({\bf x}_{O})|^{2}
and
|\tilde{\psi}_{O}({\bf v}_{O})|^{2}
have extremely narrow and effectively compact support.
In this limit, both the mixed states
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
) and
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
) reduce, approximately, to the
same
density matrix, which is simply the density matrix of the canonical pure state of Bob’s system, as seen by the CRF
O
,
\ket{\phi_{B}}\bra{\phi_{B}}_{B}
, though just how good this approximation is will depend on how tight Alice’s positional uncertainty is, and whether or not she is heavy enough to compensate for the corresponding spread in momentum.

15
15

15

Specifically, we obtain
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}\,,\,\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}\rightarrow\ket{\phi_{B}}\bra{\phi_{B}}_{B}
as
|\psi_{O}({\bf x}_{O})|^{2}\rightarrow\delta^{3}({\bf x}_{O})
and
|\tilde{\psi}_{O}({\bf v}_{O})|^{2}\rightarrow\delta^{3}({\bf v}_{O})
(
M_{O}\rightarrow\infty
), but the correct pure state is obtained from the limits of the corresponding state vectors,
\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}\,,\,\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf v}_{O})}_{B}\rightarrow\ket{\phi_{B}}_{B}
, as integration over the aforementioned probability distributions, in Eqs. (
3.77
) and (
3.86
), sets
{\bf x}_{O}=0
and
{\bf v}_{O}=0
in Eqs. (
3.75
) and (
3.87
), respectively.

In our QRF model, pure quantum states exist
objectively
in the classical background geometry but these can only be perceived as pure, with complete accuracy, by classical observers (i.e., by CRFs defined by the background itself
[
62
,
91
]
).
By contrast, all physical observers perceive pure states as mixed.
However, as the quantum subsystem that acts as a QRF becomes ‘more classical’ (i.e., larger and heavier), it more accurately perceives the nature of its fellow subsystems.
In this sense, taking the
M_{O}\rightarrow\infty
limit of Eqs. (
3.77
) and (
3.86
) provides a model of the effective quantum-to-classical transition, for a designated ‘observer’, and shows how this observer’s perception of the ‘observed’ quantum subsystem changes, accordingly.

3.3
Gedanken experiment with a tripartite system

We may construct a model in which a single subsystem of a tripartite state acts as a QRF by complete analogy with the bipartite state analysis, given in Sec.
3.2
.
Here, we use the bold face letters
({\bf r}_{A/B/C},\bm{\kappa}_{A/B/C})
to denote the phase space coordinates of Alice, Bob and Charlie, as seen by an arbitrary CRF
O
,
({\bf x}_{O/B/C},{\bf p}_{O/B/C})
to denote the coordinates of
O
, Bob and Charlie, as seen by Alice,
({\bf q}_{O/A/C},\bm{\pi}_{O/A/C})
to denote the coordinates of
O
, Alice and Charlie, as seen by Bob, and
({\bf a}_{O/A/B},\bm{\alpha}_{O/A/B})
to denote the coordinates of
O
, Alice and Bob, as seen by Charlie, as shown in Fig. 6.
The transition CRF
O

\rightarrow
QRF
A
corresponds to the coordinate transformation

\displaystyle{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf x}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{A}\,,\quad{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf x}_{B}={\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A}\,,\quad{\bf r}_{C}\mapsto{\bf x}_{C}={\bf r}_{C}-{\bf r}_{A}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto{\bf p}_{O}=-\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto{\bf p}_{B}=\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{C}\mapsto{\bf p}_{C}=\bm{\kappa}_{C}-\bm{\kappa}_{A}\,,

(3.101)

the transition CRF
O

\rightarrow
QRF
B
corresponds to

\displaystyle{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf q}_{A}=-({\bf r}_{B}-{\bf r}_{A})\,,\quad{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf q}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{B}\,,\quad{\bf r}_{C}\mapsto{\bf q}_{C}={\bf r}_{C}-{\bf r}_{AB}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto\bm{\pi}_{A}=-(\bm{\kappa}_{B}-\bm{\kappa}_{A})\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto\bm{\pi}_{O}=-\bm{\kappa}_{B}\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{C}\mapsto\bm{\pi}_{C}=\bm{\kappa}_{C}-\bm{\kappa}_{B}\,,

(3.102)

and the transition CRF
O

\rightarrow
QRF
C
corresponds to

\displaystyle{\bf r}_{A}\mapsto{\bf a}_{A}=-({\bf r}_{C}-{\bf r}_{A})\,,\quad{\bf r}_{B}\mapsto{\bf a}_{B}=-({\bf r}_{C}-{\bf r}_{B})\,,\quad{\bf r}_{C}\mapsto{\bf a}_{O}=-{\bf r}_{C}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{\kappa}_{A}\mapsto\bm{\alpha}_{A}=-(\bm{\kappa}_{C}-\bm{\kappa}_{A})\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{B}\mapsto\bm{\alpha}_{B}=-(\bm{\kappa}_{C}-\bm{\kappa}_{B})\,,\quad\bm{\kappa}_{C}\mapsto\bm{\alpha}_{O}=-\bm{\kappa}_{C}\,.

(3.103)

Figure 6
:
A tripartite state, consisting of quantum subsystems
A
,
B
and
C
, viewed from the perspective of an arbitrary classical observer,
O
, in both position and momentum space.

The composite state of the ABC system, from
O
’s perspective, is

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}=\int\int\int\psi_{A}({\bf r}_{A})\phi_{B}({\bf r}_{B})\xi_{C}({\bf r}_{C})\ket{{\bf r}_{A}}_{A}\ket{{\bf r}_{B}}_{B}\ket{{\bf r}_{C}}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm r}_{C}

\displaystyle=\int\int\int\tilde{\psi}_{A}(\bm{\kappa}_{A})\tilde{\phi}_{B}(\bm{\kappa}_{B})\tilde{\xi}_{C}(\bm{\kappa}_{C})\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{A}}_{A}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{B}}_{B}\ket{\bm{\kappa}_{C}}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{A}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm\kappa}_{C}\,,

(3.104)

which is equivalent to the density operator

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{ABC}^{(O)}:=\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}\bra{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}\,,

(3.105)

where we again assume separability, for the sake of simplicity, and the states
\ket{\Psi_{OBC}^{(A)}}_{OBC}
,
\ket{\Psi_{AOC}^{(B)}}_{AOC}
and
\ket{\Psi_{ABO}^{(C)}}_{ABO}
are obtained by acting on (
3.3
) with the relevant parity swap operators.
The probabilities associated with the outcomes of physical measurements, from the perspective of the QRFs
A
,
B
or
C
, are then determined by performing an appropriate projection, followed by the relevant partial trace,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P(\Phi_{BC}^{(A)}|\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}):=S_{OABC}^{O\rightarrow A}(\Phi_{BC}^{(A)})\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}:={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob-Charlie}=\Phi_{BC}^{(A)}}_{OBC}}\hat{\rho}_{OBC}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{6}{\rm V}_{\Phi}\,,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P(\Phi_{AC}^{(B)}|\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}):=S_{OABC}^{O\rightarrow B}(\Phi_{AC}^{(B)})\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}:={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob-Charlie}=\Phi_{AC}^{(B)}}_{AOC}}\hat{\rho}_{AOC}^{(B)}\right]{\rm d}^{6}{\rm V}_{\Phi}\,,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P(\Phi_{AB}^{(C)}|\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}):=S_{OABC}^{O\rightarrow C}(\Phi_{AB}^{(C)})\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}:={\rm tr}_{O}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{{\rm Bob-Charlie}=\Phi_{AB}^{(C)}}_{ABO}}\hat{\rho}_{ABO}^{(C)}\right]{\rm d}^{6}{\rm V}_{\Phi}\,.

We may determine the probabilities associated with the results of simultaneous position (momentum) measurements, on the composite state of Bob and Charlie, from Alice’s perspective, by projecting onto the bases

\displaystyle\left\{\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\ket{{\bf x}_{C}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{C}\right\}\quad(\left\{\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\ket{{\bf p}_{C}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{C}\right\})\,.

(3.107)

Likewise, we may determine the probabilities associated with simultaneous position (momentum) measurements, on the composite state of Alice and Charlie, from Bob’s perspective, by projecting onto

\displaystyle\left\{\ket{{\bf q}_{A}-{\bf q}_{O}}_{A}\ket{{\bf q}_{O}}_{O}\ket{{\bf q}_{C}-{\bf q}_{O}}_{C}\right\}\quad(\left\{\ket{\bm{\pi}_{A}-\bm{\pi}_{O}}_{A}\ket{\bm{\pi}_{O}}_{O}\ket{\bm{\pi}_{C}-\bm{\pi}_{O}}_{C}\right\})\,.

(3.108)

Lastly, we may determine the probabilities associated with simultaneous position (momentum) measurements, on the composite state of Alice and Bob, from Charlie’s perspective, by projecting onto

\displaystyle\left\{\ket{{\bf a}_{A}-{\bf a}_{O}}_{A}\ket{{\bf a}_{B}-{\bf a}_{O}}_{B}\ket{{\bf a}_{O}}_{O}\right\}\quad(\left\{\ket{\bm{\alpha}_{A}-\bm{\alpha}_{O}}_{A}\ket{\bm{\alpha}_{B}-\bm{\alpha}_{O}}_{B}\ket{\bm{\alpha}_{O}}_{O}\right\})\,.

(3.109)

The explicit calculation for the determination of
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C}|\Psi_{OBC}^{(A)})
, for which we introduce the shorthand notation
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})
, for simplicity, is

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})

(3.110)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\ket{{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{B}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{C}-{\bf x}_{O}}\bra{{\bf x}_{C}-{\bf x}_{O}}_{C}\hat{\rho}_{OBC}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int|\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}|\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C}-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\right){\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\psi_{O}|^{2}*(|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}|\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C})|^{2})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}\,,

where the notation
|\psi_{O}|^{2}*(|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}|\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C})|^{2})
indicates that
|\psi_{O}|^{2}
is convolved with the joint distribution
|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}|\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C})|^{2}
, through integration over a single dummy variable, but that
|\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}
and
|\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C})|^{2}
are not convolved with each other, since each is translated by the same parameter in the convolution, in this case
{\bf x}_{O}
.

16
16

16

This should not be confused with either
|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}*|\xi_{C}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B})
or
|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}*|\xi_{C}|^{2}({\bf x}_{C})
. The former requires integration over both
{\bf x}_{O}
and
{\bf x}_{C}
, whereas the latter requires integration over both
{\bf x}_{O}
and
{\bf x}_{B}
.

The same probability density can also be obtained, by complete analogy with our previous analysis of the bipartite state, by acting with the canonical projection
\ket{{\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C}}\bra{{\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C}}_{BC}
on the density operator of the effective
BC
state, as seen by
A
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{BC}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}}\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}\otimes\ket{\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{O})}\bra{\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{O})}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,,

(3.111)

where
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})=|\psi_{O}(-{\bf x}_{O})|^{2}
(
3.78
),
\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf x}_{O})}_{B}
is given by (
3.75
), and

\displaystyle\ket{\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{O})}_{C}:=\hat{U}_{C}({\bf x}_{O})\ket{\xi_{C}}_{C}:=\int\xi_{C}({\bf x}_{C}-{\bf x}_{O})\ket{{\bf x}_{C}}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}\,.

(3.112)

However, here, an additional subtlety arises that was not present in the case of the bipartite quantum system.
If Alice performs separate measurements of
{\bf x}_{B}
and
{\bf x}_{C}
, on different copies of identically prepared states in an ensemble, then the tripartite system reduces, in effect, to two distinct bipartite systems,
AB
and
AC
.
The former is analysed in Sec.
3.2
, above, and gives rise to the mixed state
\hat{\rho}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.77
), together with the corresponding GUR (
3.81
).
The
AC
system then gives rise to completely analogous expressions, namely

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{C}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}}\ket{\xi_{C}^{(A)}({\bf x}_{O})}\bra{\xi_{C}^{(A)}({\bf x}_{O})}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{O}\,,

(3.113)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rho}x_{C}^{i}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}x_{C}^{i})^{2}+(\Delta_{\xi}x_{C}^{i})^{2}}\,.

(3.114)

In this case, the probabilities of obtaining the relative displacements
{\bf x}_{B}
and
{\bf x}_{C}
are independent,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\phi_{B}|^{2}({\bf x}_{B})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}\,,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{C})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\psi_{O}|^{2}*|\xi_{C}|^{2}({\bf x}_{C})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}\,.

(3.115)

We then see, immediately, that

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})\neq{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{C})\,,

(3.116)

where
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})
is given by Eq. (
3.110
).

This result can be compared with the analogous results for both a classical observer in canonical QM and for a QRF in the GCB model
[
14
]
.
First, we note that in the canonical theory it is
always
possible to prepare a bipartite pure state that is separable, from the perspective of a given CRF
O
.
In this case, the joint-probability distribution
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})
, which represents the probability of obtaining the measured values
{\bf x}_{B}

and

{\bf x}_{C}
, is related to the individual (marginal) probability distributions via

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})={\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{C})\,.

(3.117)

In particular, we note that this relation holds true
regardless
of whether the measurements are performed simultaneously, on a single state in an ensemble, or separately, on different states therein.
Second, we note that in the GCB model a tripartite quantum state is described by only two wave functions.
Hence, every tripartite state in the GCB formalism has the same mathematical form as
some
bipartite state in canonical QM, and vice versa.
It is, therefore,
always
possible to prepare a state
BC
that is separable, from the perspective of a given QRF
A
.
For such states, Eq. (
3.117
) also holds, regardless of whether the measurements are performed simultaneously, on a single state in an ensemble, or separately on different states.

These two inequivalent measurement procedures are illustrated in Figs. 7(a) and 7(b), respectively, and it is clear that they suggest a simple experimental procedure to discriminate between competing QRF models.
In the GCB formalism, if the composite state of the
BC
system, as seen by Alice, is separable, then the relevant probabilities are
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf x}_{B},{\bf x}_{C})=|\psi({\bf x}_{C})|^{2}|\phi({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}
and
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{B})=|\phi({\bf x}_{B})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{B}
,
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf x}_{C})=|\psi({\bf x}_{C})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{C}
, which clearly obey the relation (
3.117
).
By contrast, in our model, Eq. (
3.117
) holds only for separate measurements, on different states in an ensemble, and
cannot
hold for simultaneous measurements on a single tripartite state, regardless of how we prepare the initial states of the subsystems
A
,
B
and
C
.

If the wave functions of Alice, Bob and Charlie exist objectively in the classical background space
[
62
,
91
]
, as we propose here, these different experimental procedures give rise to different joint-probability densities because the former projects pairs of wave functions onto pairs of position eigenstates, whereas the latter projects three wave functions onto three position eigenstates, at the same time.
The relation (
3.117
) is then satisfied, for simultaneous measurements, only in the limit
M_{O}\equiv M_{A}\rightarrow\infty
.
In this limit, there is again no distinction between separate and simultaneous measurements, since Alice’s positional uncertainty tends to zero (
|\psi_{O}|^{2}\equiv|\psi_{A}|^{2}\rightarrow\delta^{3}(-{\bf x}_{O})
) and the effective mixed-state density matrix
\hat{\rho}_{BC}^{(A)}
(
3.111
) reduces to canonical pure-state form,
\hat{\rho}_{BC}^{(A)}\rightarrow\hat{\rho}_{BC}=\ket{\phi_{B}}\bra{\phi_{B}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\xi_{C}}\bra{\xi_{C}}_{C}
, which is valid for a classical observer.

In the momentum space representation, the explicit calculation for the determination of the probability density
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C}|\Psi_{OBC}^{(A)})
, for which we introduce the shorthand notation
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C})
, proceeds as

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C})

(3.118)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{O}\left[\ket{{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{O}}_{O}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{B}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{C}-{\bf p}_{O}}\bra{{\bf p}_{C}-{\bf p}_{O}}_{C}\hat{\rho}_{OBC}^{(A)}\right]{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{O})|^{2}|\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B}-{\bf p}_{O})|^{2}|\tilde{\xi}_{C}({\bf p}_{C}-{\bf p}_{O})|^{2}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\right){\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*(|\tilde{\phi}_{B}({\bf p}_{B})|^{2}|\tilde{\xi}_{C}({\bf p}_{C})|^{2})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{C}\,.

The same probability density can be obtained by acting with the canonical projection operator
\ket{{\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C}}\bra{{\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C}}_{BC}
on the density matrix

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{BC}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}}\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}\bra{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}_{B}\otimes\ket{\xi_{C}({\bf p}_{O})}\bra{\xi_{C}({\bf p}_{O})}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,,

(3.119)

where
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf p}_{O})|^{2}
(
3.89
),
\ket{\phi_{B}({\bf p}_{O})}_{B}
is given by (
3.87
), and

\displaystyle\ket{\xi_{C}({\bf p}_{O})}_{C}:=\hat{\tilde{U}}_{C}({\bf p}_{O})\ket{\xi_{C}}_{C}:=\int\xi_{C}({\bf p}_{C}-{\bf p}_{O})\ket{{\bf p}_{C}}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{C}\,,

(3.120)

by complete analogy with our previous analysis for position measurements.

If, instead, Alice performs separate measurements of
{\bf p}_{B}
and
{\bf p}_{C}
, on different systems in an ensemble of identically prepared states, the tripartite system again reduces, in effect, to two bipartite systems,
AB
and
AC
.
Performing measurements of
{\bf p}_{B}
on the former gives rise to the mixed state
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{B}^{(A)}
(
3.86
), together with the corresponding GUR (
3.92
), and perfomring measurements of
{\bf p}_{C}
on the latter generates completely analogous expressions,

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{C}^{(A)}:=\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{O})}{{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}}\ket{\xi_{C}^{(A)}({\bf p}_{O})}\bra{\xi_{C}^{(A)}({\bf p}_{O})}_{C}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{O}\,,

(3.121)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rho}p_{C}^{i}=\sqrt{(\Delta_{\psi}p_{C}^{i})^{2}+(\Delta_{\xi}p_{C}^{i})^{2}}\,.

(3.122)

In the is case, the probabilities of obtaining the relative momenta
{\bf p}_{B}
and
{\bf p}_{C}
are independent,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\phi}_{B}|^{2}({\bf p}_{B})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{B}\,,

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{C})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{\psi}_{O}|^{2}*|\tilde{\xi}_{C}|^{2}({\bf p}_{C})\,{\rm d}^{3}{\rm p}_{C}\,.

(3.123)

It follows immediately that

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C})\neq{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{C})\,,

(3.124)

where
{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C})
is given by Eq. (
3.118
).

This result should also be compared with the analogous results for a classical observer in canonical QM and for a QRF in the GCB model
[
14
]
.
In the canonical theory the relevant probability distributions for a bipartite, separable, pure quantum state
BC
obey the relation

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{6}P({\bf p}_{B},{\bf p}_{C})={\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{B}){\rm d}^{3}P({\bf p}_{C})\,,

(3.125)

regardless of whether the measurements are performed separately, on different states in an ensemble, or simultaneously on a single state.
A completely analogous relation therefore holds, in the GCB formalism, for all
BC
states that appear separable from the perspective of the QRF
A
.

In our QRF model, the relation (
3.125
) can only satisfied be satisfied in the limit
M_{O}\equiv M_{A}\rightarrow\infty
, when the mixed state density matrix for momentum-measurements,
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{BC}^{(A)}
(
3.119
), reduces to the density matrix for the objective pure state,
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{BC}^{(A)}\rightarrow\hat{\rho}_{BC}=\ket{\phi_{B}}\bra{\phi_{B}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\xi_{B}}\bra{\xi_{B}}_{B}
.
To see this, we need only rewrite (
3.119
) as an integral over the velocity-space volume element,
{\rm d}^{3}{\rm v}_{O}
, and note that
{\rm d}^{3}P({\bf v}_{O})/{\rm d}^{3}{\rm v}_{O}=|\tilde{\psi}_{O}(-{\bf v}_{O})|^{2}\rightarrow\delta^{3}(-{\bf v}_{O})
, for
M_{O}\rightarrow\infty
.
Hence, in this limit, there is no distinction between separate and simultaneous measurements, of either position or momentum, since Alice’s position and velocity uncertainties both tend to zero, and she becomes ‘fully classical’, as expected.

Finally, we note that the generalised position and momentum operators, representing Alice’s measurements on the
BC
subsystem of the objective tripartite state
\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}
(
3.3
), can be denoted as
\hat{X}_{B/C}^{i}:=\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B/C}^{i}+\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{B/C}^{\prime i}
and
\hat{P}_{B/Cj}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{B/Cj}+\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{B/Cj}^{\prime}
, respectively.
These are constructed, explicitly, via a straightforward extension of the definitions (
3.57
) and (
3.58
), given in Sec.
3.2
.
By exponentiating commuting pairs of the sub-operators
\left\{\hat{{\bf\mathcal{X}}}_{B/C},\hat{{\bf\mathcal{X}}}_{B/C}^{\prime},\hat{{\bf\mathcal{P}}}_{B/C},\hat{{\bf\mathcal{P}}}_{B/C}^{\prime}\right\}
we can again construct useful unitary operators that shuffle between entangled and unentangled bases of
\ket{\Psi_{ABC}^{(O)}}_{ABC}
(
3.3
), in whatever coordinate system we wish to employ,
({\bf r}_{A/B/C},\bm{\kappa}_{A/B/C})
,
({\bf x}_{O/B/C},{\bf p}_{O/B/C})
,
({\bf q}_{O/A/C},\bm{\pi}_{O/A/C})
or
({\bf a}_{O/A/B},\bm{\alpha}_{O/A/B})
.

We stress, however, that such transformations do not change the fundamental relations between these coordinate systems.
All such unitarily equivalent bases are consistent with the perspective of the CRF
O
, and with the relevant coordinate transformation, (
3.3
), (
3.3
) or (
3.3
), for whichever coordinates we choose to use.
Generalised operators representing Bob’s measurements on the
AC
subsystem and Charlie’s measurements on the
AB
subsystem can also be constructed in like manner, although the corresponding proliferation of notation becomes rather cumbersome.
We address this issue in our treatment of the general
N
-partite system, given in Sec.
3.4
.

Figure 7
:
Fig. 7(a) (left) illustrates an experimental procedure in which separate measurements of
{\bf x}_{B}
and
{\bf x}_{C}
are performed on different states in an ensemble, causing Alice’s wave function to collapse, in general, to different position eigenstates. Fig. 7(b) (right) illustrates the simultaneous measurement of
{\bf x}_{B}
and
{\bf x}_{C}
on a single state of the tripartite system. These procedures generate different joint-probability distribtutions, which can be used to differentiate between competing definitions of the term ‘quantum reference frame’. An analogous diagram can also be constructed for measurements of the relative momenta,
{\bf p}_{B}
and
{\bf p}_{C}
. In these figures, we indicate the regions in which the wave functions of
A
,
B
and
C
are nonzero with three closed curves, but neglect to shade their interiors, so that the different configurations to which the subsystems collapse can be seen more clearly.

3.4
Generalisation to
N
-particle states

In generalising our treatment of the tripartite state to a general
N
-partite system, for arbitrarily large
N
, the biggest challenge we face is that of choosing an appropriate notation.
For
N\gtrsim 10^{2}
, it is not only cumbersome, but also impracticable, to use different Latin or Greek letters (and variants thereof) to denote the different coordinates employed by different subsystems.

We therefore choose, instead, to label the coordinates used by an arbitrary CRF with a zero, rather than an
O
, and to label those employed by quantum subsystems numerically as
1,2,\dots N
.
The corresponding phase space coordinates are written explicitly as
({\bf x}_{(0)}^{[m]},{\bf p}_{(0)}^{[m]})
and
({\bf x}_{(m)}^{[n]},{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[n]})
, respectively, for
m\in\left\{1,2,\dots N\right\}
and
n\in\left\{0,1,2,\dots N\right\}
, with
n\neq m
.
In other words, we use round brackets to indicate the subsystem that is acting as the QRF and square brackets to label all other subsystems.
The individual components of these displacements and momenta can then be labelled using unbracketed suffixes, as usual, i.e., as
({\bf x}_{(0)}^{[m]i},{\bf p}_{(0)j}^{[m]})
and
({\bf x}_{(m)}^{[n]i},{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[n]j})
, where
i,j\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
.
In this notation, the objective state of the
N
-partite system, from the perspective of CRF-0, is written as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{1,2,\dots N}^{(0)}}_{1,2,\dots N}=\int\int\dots\int\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\psi_{n}({\bf x}_{(0)}^{[n]})\ket{{\bf x}_{(0)}^{[n]}}_{n}{\rm d}^{3}{\rm x}_{(0)}^{[n]}\,.

(3.126)

Again, we assume the state is separable, from the perspective of the classical observer, for simplicity.

The probabilities associated with the outcomes of physical measurements, from the perspective of the QRF-
m
, for an arbitrary value of
m
in the range
2<m<N
, are then given by

\displaystyle{\rm d}^{3(N-1)}P(\Phi_{1,2,\dots m-1,m+1,\dots N}^{(m)}|\Psi_{1,2,\dots N}^{(0)})

(3.127)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{0,1,2,\dots N}^{(0\rightarrow m)}(\Phi_{1,2,\dots m-1,m+1,\dots N}^{(m)})\,\ket{\Psi_{1,2,\dots N}^{(0)}}_{1,2,\dots N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}_{0}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\ket{\Phi_{1,2,\dots m-1,m+1,\dots N}^{(m)}}_{1,2,\dots N}}\hat{\rho}_{1,2,\dots m-1,0,m+1,\dots N}^{(m)}\right]{\rm d}^{3(N-1)}{\rm V}_{\Phi}\,,

where the partial trace is taken over the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{(m)}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{0\leftrightarrow m}\,\mathcal{H}_{m}^{(0)}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{m}
of the total Hilbert space of the
N
-partite system.
Those for
m=1,2
and
N
are defined in like manner, with index addition taken modulo
N
, but we select
m
in the range
2<m<N
, to illustrate our procedure.
Hence, in order to determine probability density associated with simultaneous measurements of the
N-1
relative displacements,
{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[1]}
,
{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[2]}
, . . , we must project onto the bases

\displaystyle\left\{\ket{{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[1]}-{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{1}\dots\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[m-1]}-{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{m-1}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{0}\otimes\ket{{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[m+1]}-{\bf x}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{m+1}\dots\right\}\,,

(3.128)

whereas, to determine the probability density associated with simultaneous measurements of the
N-1
relative momenta,
{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[1]}
,
{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[2]}
, . . , we must project onto the bases

\displaystyle\left\{\ket{{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[1]}-{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{1}\dots\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[m-1]}-{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{m-1}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{0}\otimes\ket{{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[m+1]}-{\bf p}_{(m)}^{[0]}}_{m+1}\dots\right\}\,.

(3.129)

The generalised operators, corresponding to measurements of relative position and momentum, performed by the QRF-
m
, are denoted as

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{(m)}^{[n]i}:=\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}^{[n]i}+\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}^{\prime[n]i}\,,\quad\hat{P}_{(m)j}^{[n]}:=\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{(m)j}^{[n]}+\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{(m)j}^{\prime[n]}\,,

(3.130)

and the definitions of the sub-operators
\left\{\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}^{[n]i},\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{(m)}^{\prime[n]i},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{(m)j}^{[n]},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{(m)j}^{\prime[n]}\right\}
should be obvious to the reader who has patiently followed our arguments thus far.
Using these basic ingredients, the density operators of the effective mixed states seen by an arbitrary QRF, labelled QRF-
m
, in both position and momentum space, can be constructed, and it is straightforward to show that these reduce to the density operator of the objective pure state seen by CRF-
0
- absent the
\mathcal{H}_{m}
subsystem, which has been traced out - in the limit
M_{0}\equiv M_{m}\rightarrow\infty
.

4
